Incidence of occupational HIV seroconversion among healthcare workers: a systematic review and meta-analysis
Authors: Ana Carolina Marques-Medeiros, Livia Guimarães Zina, Lucas Guimarães Abreu, Suellen Rocha Mendes, Cristiane Menezes Pádua, Rafaela Silveira Pinto, Mauro Henrique Nogueira Guimarães Abreu
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
BackgroundHealthcare workers (HCWs) may be at elevated risk of HIV infection due to the global HIV burden and frequent occupational exposure to blood and body fluids (BBF). This systematic review aimed to estimate the global incidence of occupational HIV seroconversion among HCWs by synthesizing evidence from observational studies.MethodsThis review followed PRISMAguidelines and was registered in PROSPERO. Systematic searches were conductedin six major databases and gray literature to identify observational studies reporting HIV seroconversion following occupational exposure. Studies were included regardless of whether the source patient’s HIV serostatus was confirmed, in line with real-world reporting practices. Data were extracted onseroconversion, PEP adherence, exposure type, and follow-up. Meta-analyses of proportions were performed using fixed-effect models with arcsine and Freeman-Tukey double arcsine transformations to account for the rarity of events. Heterogeneity was assessed using I² and tau² statistics.ResultsA total of 132 studies were included. The pooled probability of occupational HIV seroconversion across all reported exposures was 0.0125 per 1000 exposure incidents (95% CI:0.0003–0.0425). Among individuals who completed follow-up, the incidence was 0.1215 per 1000 exposure incidents (95% CI: 0.0284–0.2795). For percutaneous exposures, the pooled incidence was 0.5512 per 1000 exposure incidents (95% CI:0.1620–1.1714), and for mucocutaneous exposures, 1.7840 per 1000 exposure incidents (95% CI: 0.7584–3.2409). Among PEP users, the pooled incidence was 0.0098 per 1000 exposure incidents (95% CI: 0.0000–0.3597), compared to 1.7448per 1000 exposure incidents (95% CI: 0.2768–4.4726) among non-adherent individuals.ConclusionOccupational HIV seroconversion among HCWs is rare. Adherence to PEP is associated with reduced transmission risk, reinforcing the importance of timely follow-up and strategies in occupational settings.Systematic review registration numberPROSPERO CRD42023406192Supplementary InformationThe online version contains supplementary material available at 10.1186/s12913-026-14515-0.

Background

Healthcare workers (HCWs) may face an increased risk of HIV infection due to frequent occupational exposure to blood and body fluids (BBF) and the high global burden of HIV [1]. The 2024 UNAIDS report estimated that, in 2023, 39.9 million people were living with HIV worldwide, with an adult prevalence rate of 0.8% [2]. Studies conducted between 2015 and 2020 reported that 39.0% to 60.6% of HCWs experienced occupational exposure to BBF [3]. The risk of HIV transmission depends on the type of exposure and the use of protective measures, such as post-exposure prophylaxis (PEP) [4]. More recent findings reinforce these concerns: in Eritrea, Negash et al. [5] observed a 12-month prevalence of needlestick injuries of 37.1%, but only 10.4% of those exposed initiated PEP. Similarly, in a scoping review, Auerbach et al. [6] reported consistently low PEP uptake across low- and middle-income countries, citing barriers such as stigma, limited access, inadequate training, and incomplete adherence.

Despite increasing awareness, estimates of actual transmission risk remain limited, with few comprehensive syntheses available. Since the 1980´s, there have been reports of occupational transmission of HIV among health workers [7]. Henderson et al. [7], a figure widely cited in the literature [8–11], estimated a 0.3% transmission risk following percutaneous exposure to HIV-positive blood. Baggaley et al. [12] conducted the only prior global systematic review, using modeled data to estimate transmission probabilities of 0.32% for percutaneous exposures, 0.09% for mucosal exposures, and 0.003% for exposures involving non-intact skin. Although important at the time, their synthesis included only 21 studies on accidental exposures and did not address follow-up procedures, post-exposure prophylaxis (PEP), or regional variability. Systematic reviews are particularly valuable when primary data are fragmented or inconsistently reported, as was the case in the early 2000s. The present review builds upon that effort by incorporating a broader range of countries and healthcare contexts, and by evaluating observed seroconversion outcomes stratified by exposure type, PEP adherence, and follow-up completion. Our methods follow the current PRISMA guidelines [13] and apply the Joanna Briggs Institute (JBI) approach [14] for risk of bias assessment. Some primary studies included in Baggaley’s review were also retrieved in our search and incorporated into this synthesis when they met the updated eligibility criteria. Although seroconversion is a longitudinal outcome, we included cross-sectional studies when they reported documented exposure to HIV-positive blood or body fluids and provided post-exposure serological status. This systematic review aims to estimate the global incidence of occupational HIV seroconversion among healthcare workers following exposure to blood or body fluids considered potentially contaminated, based on follow-up procedures established by institutional or national post-exposure protocols. By synthesizing observational studies reporting real-world exposures and outcomes, this review seeks to fill an important evidence gap and to support the development of prevention strategies and improvements in occupational HIV surveillance. Hence, our research question is: What is the pooled global incidence of occupational HIV seroconversion among healthcare workers following exposure to potentially contaminated blood or body fluids?

Materials and methods

This review is registered in PROSPERO (CRD42023406192) and follows the Preferred Reporting Items for Systematic Reviews and Meta-Analysis [13] guidelines (see PRISMA checklist in Supplementary Table 1 (Additional file 1)). The Joanna Briggs Institute framework for prevalence and incidence studies was adopted as a methodological approach. The review was conducted in the following steps: systematic database search and removal of duplicate studies; peer-reviewed screening of titles and abstracts; peer-reviewed full-text selection of studies; data extraction from the selected articles; synthesis of extracted data; methodological quality assessment of the studies; evaluation of evidence certainty; and meta-analysis of the findings.

Eligibility criteria

Studies on occupational exposure to blood and body fluids (BFM) that reported on HIV seroconversion among healthcare workers (HCWs) were selected. Confounding factors, such as behaviors and personal conditions that affect the risk of HIV infection, were considered [15]. Occupational exposures to blood and body fluids (BBF) were included regardless of the HIV serostatus of the source patient. Although some studies adopted institutional or national protocols that recommended follow-up primarily for individuals exposed to sources confirmed positive or of unknown status, all reported exposures were considered to minimize selection bias. Additionally, even in cases without extended serological follow-up, exposed individuals without seroconversion were reported as part of the study population and contributed to the incidence denominator. This approach is supported by clinical guidelines from major health authorities [16–18], which state that follow-up testing is not required when the source patient is confirmed HIV-negative and the exposed individual has a baseline negative test. In such cases, the absence of monitoring does not indicate incomplete data but reflects a protocol-based decision consistent with low-risk exposure management. To ensure methodological clarity, we conducted separate meta-analyses: one including all reported exposure cases and another restricted to exposures with confirmed serological follow-up, thereby balancing real-world surveillance diversity with methodological rigor. This allowed us to reflect both the broader occupational surveillance context and the set of higher-risk exposures more consistently followed in clinical practice.

Only observational studies on HIV seroconversion following occupational exposure were included, with no language or date restrictions. Cross-sectional studies were eligible only if they retrospectively reported exposures and follow-up with documented seroconversion outcomes. These were conceptually treated as retrospective cohorts based on the temporality of data collection, regardless of the authors’ original classification. Prospective cohort studies were defined as those in which participants were followed from exposure onward, while retrospective cohorts analyzed data after both exposure and outcome had occurred, in line with Gordis’ criteria [19]. Studies included in the Baggaley et al. [12] review were re-screened according to the updated eligibility criteria of the present review and were included only if they met the predefined methodological standards. Case-control studies, clinical trials, literature reviews, case reports, editorials, and studies with insufficient data regarding methodology were excluded. Since the PICO question structure is not suited for questions related to prevalence and incidence studies, the CoCoPop acronym (Condition, Context, and Population) was chosen to guide the inclusion criteria for the studies reviewed, in accordance with methodological guidance for prevalence and incidence systematic reviews [14]. Our review evaluated HIV seroconversion rates (Condition) among healthcare professionals (Population) exposed to BBF (Context).

For this review, healthcare workers (HCWs) were defined by the framework proposed by the World Health Organization and the Global Health Workforce Alliance [20] as individuals whose primary intent is to improve health and who are either directly involved in patient care or occupationally exposed to blood or body fluids. This definition aligns with the International Standard Classification of Occupations 2008 (ISCO-08) and commonly includes physicians, nurses, midwives, dentists, and laboratory professionals. Support staff, such as cleaning personnel, were considered only when explicitly mentioned in the original studies and directly linked to occupational exposure to biological material.

Search strategy

The search strategy was designed using descriptors from MeSH (available on PUBMED), Emtree (available on Embase), and DeCS (available on BVS), combined with Boolean operators. The search terms included: 1) HIV; 2) seroconversion; 3) occupational blood exposure; and 4) healthcare professionals. This strategy was validated by two experts in systematic reviews and tailored for each search tool used in the databases. The full search strategy, including synonyms and alternative terms for all key concepts, is detailed in Supplementary Table 2 (Additional file 1).

The search was conducted across six databases: MEDLINE (via PUBMED), Embase, Scopus, Web of Science, the Cochrane Database of Systematic Reviews, and LILACS (via BVS). Additionally, gray literature was explored through Open Gray – the European information system for gray literature –, Google Scholar, and by reviewing theses and dissertations. In Google Scholar, the first 200 references retrieved were assessed according to methodological guidelines for systematic reviews [21]. Finally, the reference lists of the selected studies were examined to identify any additional studies not captured in the electronic search.

Selection process

Studies identified in the electronic search were exported to EndNote v. 20.0 (Clarivate Analytics, PA, USA), and duplicate references were removed. Study screening was performed using Rayyan v. 2023 (Rayyan Systems Inc., MA, USA). Two researchers independently evaluated titles and abstracts based on the eligibility criteria. For studies with insufficient information in the titles and abstracts, full texts were retrieved for further assessment, and authors were contacted if additional data were needed. The reviewers reviewed the full texts of selected studies for final inclusion, and a summary of the reasons for inclusion and exclusion for the selected studies is provided in Fig. 1. Discrepancies at any stage were resolved through discussion and consensus. Researchers underwent training and calibration before the selection process - using a 10% sample of the retrieved studies - with a third reviewer (either a senior researcher) serving as the gold standard. Intra- and inter-rater agreement levels above 80% were deemed satisfactory. Fig. 1PRISMA flow diagram

PRISMA flow diagram

Data collection process, data items, and effect measures

The data extraction process included the following information: study design, country and location of the research, language and publication date; sample size, individual characteristics of the population studied (professional category, age, and sex of participants); details of occupational exposure (site of the accident, type of sharp material, type of injury, contaminated biological material); indication and adherence to post-exposure prophylaxis (PEP), type of PEP used, and the time elapsed between exposure to biological material and PEP adoption; HIV seroconversion rates among participants, comparison of seroconversion rates based on PEP adherence, and follow-up period; outcome measures (methods for assessing seroconversion, incidence measures such as proportions/rates/indices), as well as data on dropouts, missing data, presence of bias, and control of confounding factors such as socioeconomic conditions, prior health status, comorbidities, access to healthcare services, and type of care. For studies with missing data, researchers contacted the authors to obtain additional information, when possible.

Although this review followed the Joanna Briggs Institute (JBI) framework for reviews of prevalence and incidence data, the outcome analyzed (HIV seroconversion) is conceptually a measure of cumulative incidence, as it reflects the proportion of initially disease-free individuals who develop infection over time [19]. Therefore, despite the alignment with JBI methods, the terminology used in this manuscript was adapted to reflect the longitudinal nature of the outcome.

Regarding follow-up duration, studies were included in the meta-analysis if they reported the number of individuals who completed serological follow-up, and if the follow-up period, based on the study protocol, was sufficient to detect HIV seroconversion. Although follow-up intervals varied, often based on local or institutional post-exposure protocols, all included studies ensured a temporal window appropriate for identifying new infections. We recognize that these variations may introduce heterogeneity, which was considered in the certainty of evidence assessment and explored through group and sensitivity analyses.

Synthesis methods

Data pooling was conducted using the Rstudio software (Rstudio, Boston, Massachusetts, USA) with R programming language, employing the meta package [22]. Data of studies were pooled into meta-analyses of proportion. Given that the incidence rate of individuals exhibiting seroconversion was very low and often zero within studies, which leads to minimal variance and may overweight studies with no events, fixed-effect models were applied. Arcsine and Freeman–Tukey double arcsine transformations were used to stabilize variance estimates [23, 24]. Although this transformation does not intrinsically address the shortcomings of zero-event studies, it allows one to incorporate the data of those studies into the analysis without the need for arbitrary continuity corrections, such as adding 0.5 to empty cells, thus precluding the artificial inflation of variance [23, 24]. Following the recommendations of the Cochrane Handbook for Systematic Reviews, a complementary sensitivity analysis excluding studies with zero seroconversion events was also conducted. The summary measures were reported in terms of the pooled proportion and 95% confidence interval (CI). To assess heterogeneity, the I2 method was used to evaluate the variability of the effect size, and tau-square (τ2) was employed to assess between-study variance. For I2, a 95% CI was calculated [25]. The Maximum-Likelihood method was deployed.

Publication bias was assessed using funnel plots [26] and the Egger test [27]. Given the known limitations of these tools in rare-event meta-analyses and when the number of studies is small, findings were interpreted with caution. Funnel plots were constructed using the transformed proportions (Freeman–Tukey double arcsine), and asymmetry may reflect small-study effects, sparse data with zero-event studies, or methodological heterogeneity rather than true publication bias. Therefore, these assessments were treated as exploratory and contextualized within the broader limitations of the evidence base [26, 27].

Although included studies reported seroconversion using diverse epidemiological metrics, all effect measures were standardized as cumulative incidence proportions. These proportions were computed as the number of seroconversions divided by the most appropriate denominator for each analysis, including total reported exposures, exposures with follow-up, exposure type (e.g., percutaneous or mucocutaneous), and PEP indication and use.

Following guidance from the Cochrane Handbook [28] and Borenstein et al. [29], we performed separate meta-analyses for each clinically distinct exposure context, instead of conducting subgroup analysis within a single model. This approach was warranted due to the biological, clinical, and methodological differences among the exposure types. For example, mucocutaneous and percutaneous exposures differ in transmission risk and reporting practices, while PEP adherence may biologically reduce the risk of seroconversion [17, 30].

Whenever needed, raw data were recalculated from reported rates or indices to ensure standardization and comparability of effect measures. In meta-analyses stratified by exposure type, certain studies with very large sample sizes and no events were excluded when their inclusion led to extremely small variances, thereby distorting pooled estimates and impairing graphical interpretability. In the analysis of mucocutaneous exposures, for example, Fahey et al. [31], (n = 7,956; 0 events) was excluded based on its disproportionate influence on the variance structure. This decision followed established methodological guidance for rare-event meta-analyses.

Although data were extracted on healthcare setting, geographic region, and professional category, variations in terminology and incomplete reporting across studies limited the feasibility of subgroup meta-analyses for these variables. These contextual elements were instead incorporated into the descriptive synthesis and are further addressed in the Discussion.

In addition to stratified analyses by exposure type, follow-up status, and PEP use, a sensitivity analysis was conducted to assess the robustness of the findings. All six meta-analyses were repeated after excluding studies classified as high risk of bias based on the JBI Critical Appraisal Tool. The same analytical procedures, including fixed-effect models and Freeman–Tukey transformations, were applied to this restricted subset. This approach aimed to identify whether any specific results would change after excluding lower-quality studies, by repeating the same analytical steps using a restricted dataset. The results of the sensitivity analysis are presented in the Results section and illustrated in Supplementary Figures.

Additionally, group analyses were conducted to examine whether HIV prevalence in the countries where the studies had been conducted influenced the observed seroconversion rates. For each included study, national estimates of adult HIV prevalence (aged 15–49 years or general population, depending on data availability) corresponding to the study year were retrieved primarily from UNAIDS and World Bank databases. When data for specific years or countries were unavailable, estimates were complemented with information from official national surveillance reports and additional published research. All extracted prevalence data and their corresponding sources are detailed in Supplementary Table 3 (Additional file 1).

Studies were then grouped according to national HIV prevalence: those conducted in countries with HIV prevalence below 1% (representing 92.4% of all included studies) and those conducted in countries with HIV prevalence above 1%. Among studies with lower-prevalence settings, two additional groups were generated: one including all studies, and another restricted to studies reporting cases of seroconversion, to allow targeted sensitivity testing of observed events. In high-prevalence settings, a separate dataset was prepared; however, none of the studies in this group reported seroconversion; thus, no additional stratified analysis was applicable.

Risk of bias assessment

Two independent reviewers assessed the risk of bias (RoB) using the Joanna Briggs Institute (JBI) Critical Appraisal Tool for prevalence and incidence studies [14], which comprises nine domains rated as “yes,” “no,” “not applicable,” or “unclear” Discrepancies were resolved through discussion and consensus, and the reviewers were previously trained and calibrated with the support of a senior researcher. All studies, regardless of their assessed risk of bias, were included in data extraction and, when appropriate, in the meta-analysis. The RoB domains were interpreted considering the context of occupational exposures to blood and body fluids (BBF), which often follow institutional or national protocols with variability in follow-up. Each checklist item scored “yes” received 1 point, and the proportion of positive responses was used to classify the study’s overall risk of bias. Greater weight was assigned to domains 5, 6, 7, and 9, which assess the representativeness of the sample, the reliability of outcome measures (i.e., HIV seroconversion), and the completeness of follow-up. Studies that met ≥ 70% of the checklist items were classified as low RoB, those that met 50–69% as moderate RoB, and those below 50% as high RoB. Additionally, we reported the proportion of studies that met each individual domain to improve transparency and identify patterns of potential bias across studies. Although percentage thresholds were used for classification, domain-level judgments were also qualitatively reviewed to ensure that critical sources of bias were not masked by high overall scores. This approach allowed us to consider not only the quantity but also the relevance of the domains met when judging each study’s contribution to the evidence base

Certainty assessment

The certainty of evidence was assessed using the Grading of Recommendations Assessment, Development and Evaluation (GRADE) framework [32]. Evidence was classified into four levels—high, moderate, low, and very low—based on evaluation across five domains: risk of bias (e.g., serious methodological limitations), inconsistency (e.g., substantial variability in study results), indirectness (evidence not directly applicable to the CoCoPop question), imprecision (e.g., wide confidence intervals or small sample sizes), and publication bias (e.g., suspected selective reporting). Observational studies were initially considered low-certainty evidence and could be downgraded further based on these domains. Although the GRADE approach was originally developed for systematic reviews of interventions (e.g., randomized controlled trials), it has been increasingly adapted to assess certainty in observational studies reporting prevalence and incidence outcomes. This broader application is supported by guidance from the Joanna Briggs Institute, which recognizes the appropriateness of applying GRADE to evidence derived from observational studies estimating measures of frequency such as incidence and prevalence [33]. Following this adaptation, we assessed the certainty of cumulative incidence estimates across the included observational studies.

Results

Studies selection and characteristics

A total of 1,056 articles were retrieved from the databases, and 369 were identified through article references. Out of these, 132 articles were selected by the two reviewers. The flowchart of the selection process is shown in Fig. 1, with detailed reasons for inclusion and exclusion provided in Table 1. In the descriptive synthesis (Table 2), most studies were in English (n = 100, 75.8%); 49 (37.1%) were prospective cohort studies, 82 (62.1%) were retrospective and one was a study with both designs. The geographical distribution of studies was classified according to the United Nations geoscheme [34]. The majority were conducted in the European region (n = 39; 29.5%), followed by the Americas (n = 31; 23.5%), South Asia (n = 16; 12.1%), and West Asia (n = 16; 12.1%). Fewer studies were identified from regions such as Western Africa (n = 4; 3.0%), Southeast Asia (n = 2; 1.5%), and Latin America and the Caribbean (n = 11; 8.3%). *Retrospective Cohort; †Prospective Cohort; ‡Retrospective and Prospective Cohort

The number of reported BBF exposures ranged from 5 to 43,464 with 1 to 7,956 completing follow-up. The age of exposed individuals varied from 17 to 74 years, with women being the most affected (n = 125, 94.7%). Percutaneous injuries accounted for 96.8% (n = 128) of studies. PEP use was reported in 80 studies (60.6%). The incidence of seroconversion among all exposures ranged from 0 per 1000 exposure incidents to 11.4 per 1000 exposure incidents. Among individuals who completed follow-up, the incidence ranged from 0 per 1000 exposure incidents to 21.7 per 1000 exposure incidents. For percutaneous exposures, it ranged from 0 per 1000 exposure incidents to 18.9 per 1000 exposure incidents, and for mucocutaneous exposures, from 0 per 1000 exposure incidents to 6.3 per 1000 exposure incidents. In studies with reports of PEP use, the incidence ranged from 0 per 1000 exposure incidents to 7.0 per 1000 exposure incidents. For those who were indicated for PEP but did not adhere, the incidence ranged from 0 per 1000 exposure incidents to 28.6 per 1000 exposure incidents.

Regarding the HIV serostatus of the source patients, among the 132 included studies, 36 (27.3%) followed only exposures to confirmed HIV-positive sources, 15 (11.4%) included exposures to HIV-positive and/or unknown-status sources, and 8 (6.1%) included exposures to HIV-negative sources. However, in 73 studies (55.3%), the status of the source was either not reported or incompletely described (Supplementary Table 4; see Additional file 1).

Risk of bias (RoB) assessment findings by type of incidence

Table 3 presents a detailed summary of the risk of bias (RoB) assessment for the individual studies included in this review. It evaluates each study across nine methodological domains, highlighting strengths and potential limitations. Additionally, the table identifies the studies included for specific outcomes analyzed in the meta-analysis. In addition to Table 3, supplementary tables provide detailed risk of bias (RoB) assessments for each specific outcome analyzed in the meta-analysis, including follow-up completion, type of exposure (percutaneous or mucocutaneous), and PEP use (or non-use). Table 3Summary of risk of bias (RoB) assessment for each study and across domains using the Joanna Briggs institute critical appraisal tool checklist for prevalence studies

Table 3Summary of risk of bias (RoB) assessment for each study and across domains using the Joanna Briggs institute critical appraisal tool checklist for prevalence studies

Summary of risk of bias (RoB) assessment for each study and across domains using the Joanna Briggs institute critical appraisal tool checklist for prevalence studies

Seroconversion among all reported occupational exposures

Among the included studies, 55.30% were classified as high quality, 38.64% as moderate, and 6.06% as low. High quality was observed in Domain 1 (sample frame − 100%) and Domain 8 (statistical analysis − 100%), ensuring representativeness and appropriate statistical methods. Domain 4 (setting description − 46.21%) and Domain 6 (valid methods − 45.45%) showed lower quality, suggesting a potential risk of bias due to incomplete contextual and methodological clarity.

Seroconversion among exposed individuals who completed follow-up

The methodological quality of the included studies showed that 63.64% were classified as high quality, 33.64% as moderate, and 2.73% as low. High quality was observed in Domain 1 (sample frame − 100%), Domain 2 (sampling − 98.18%), Domain 5 (sample coverage − 93.64%), Domain 8 (statistical analysis − 100%), and Domain 9 (response rate − 95.45%). Domain 4 (setting description − 46.36%) and Domain 6 (valid methods − 48.18%) may indicate moderate risk due to limitations in setting details and methodological rigor. Further details are available in Supplementary Table 5 (see Additional file 1).

Seroconversion by type of exposure – percutaneous

Among studies that assessed seroconversion by percutaneous exposure, 62.86% were classified as high quality, 34.29% as moderate, and 2.73% as low. High quality was demonstrated in Domain 1 (sample frame − 100%), Domain 2 (sampling − 100%), Domain 5 (sample coverage − 91.43%), Domain 8 (statistical analysis − 100%), and Domain 9 (response rate − 94.29%), providing a solid methodological foundation. Domain 4 (setting description − 42.86%) and moderate scores in Domain 6 (valid methods − 51.43%) and Domain 7 (standardized measurements − 51.43%) suggest some moderate risk of bias. Further details are available in Supplementary Table 6 (see Additional file 1).

Seroconversion by type of exposure – mucocutaneous

Among studies assessing seroconversion by mucocutaneous exposure, 63.16% were classified as high quality, 31.58% as moderate, and 5.26% as low. High quality was found in Domain 1 (sample frame − 100%), Domain 2 (sampling − 100%), Domain 5 (sample coverage − 89.47%), Domain 8 (statistical analysis − 100%), and Domain 9 (response rate − 94.74%), ensuring a robust methodological base. Moderate scores in Domain 4 (setting description − 47.37%), Domain 6 (valid methods − 47.37%), and Domain 7 (standardized measurements − 47.37%) indicate a moderate risk of bias. Further details are available in Supplementary Table 7 (see Additional file 1).

Seroconversion among a population with PEP use

Among studies evaluating seroconversion in the population using PEP, 57.45% were classified as high quality, 38.30% as moderate, and 4.26% as low. High quality was observed in Domain 1 (sample frame − 100%), Domain 2 (sampling − 100%), Domain 5 (sample coverage − 91.49%), Domain 8 (statistical analysis − 100%), and Domain 9 (response rate − 93.62%), providing a reliable methodological base. Moderate scores in Domain 4 (setting description − 40.43%) and Domains 6 and 7 (valid methods and standardized measurements - both 74.07%) suggest a moderate risk of bias. Further details are available in Supplementary Table 8 (see Additional file 1).

Seroconversion among a population with indication but without PEP use

Among studies assessing seroconversion in the population with indication but without PEP use, 57.14% were classified as high quality, 38.10% as moderate, and 4.76% as low. High quality was observed in Domain 1 (sample frame − 100%), Domain 2 (sampling − 100%), Domain 5 (sample coverage − 85.71%), Domain 8 (statistical analysis − 100%), and Domain 9 (response rate − 95.24%), ensuring a strong evidence base. Although Moderate scores in Domain 4 (setting description − 42.86%) and Domains 6 and 7 (valid methods and standardized measurements - both 47.62%) indicate a moderate risk of bias. Further details are available in Supplementary Table 9 (see Additional file 1).

Certainty assessment

Table 4 summarizes the GRADE evidence profile and corresponding justifications. For all studies, no concerning inconsistencies, indirectness, or imprecision were observed. For the overall incidence rate of seroconversion among the total population of occupationally exposed individuals, the risk of bias was assessed as very serious. This assessment was largely due to the outcome being based on the entire population of exposed individuals, rather than only those who completed the recommended period for serological status verification. Consequently, the overall certainty of the evidence was classified as very low. In contrast, for the group of individuals who completed the recommended follow-up, as well as for those exposed to percutaneous and mucocutaneous accidents, and those who used the indicated PEP, the risk of bias was deemed serious. However, this bias was considered plausible but unlikel to significantly alter the results, resulting in a classification of evidence as low. Synthesis of results by outcome

Number of individuals who presented seroconversion and total number of occupational exposures to biological material

The result of the meta-analysis demonstrated that the pooled incidence of seroconversion was 0.01248 per 1000 exposure incidents (95% CI: [0.00030–0.04248]). Heterogeneity was I2 = 0.0% [0.0%; 21.6%] and τ2 <0.0001 (Fig. 2). The funnel plot is shown in Fig. 3. The Egger test result confirmed the funnel plot’s symmetry (p = 0.416). Discussion

This systematic review and meta-analysis provide a comprehensive synthesis of HIV seroconversion incidence among healthcare professionals worldwide following occupational exposure. Although seroconversion is rare, it remains a clinically relevant outcome. Documented cases indicate that specific risk factors substantially increase the likelihood of transmission, particularly deep percutaneous injuries involving hollow-bore needles and exposures involving source patients in advanced stages of infection [35–37]. These findings are consistent with occupational safety guidelines16 and prior evidence, reinforcing the need for preventive measures and the timely use of PEP in high-risk exposure scenarios [36, 38, 39].

Even when pooled incidence estimates are low, occupational HIV seroconversion has meaningful implications, especially in contexts of repeated exposures or suboptimal post-exposure management. Importantly, low per-exposure risk does not necessarily translate into negligible individual risk over a working lifetime, since occupational exposures, particularly sharps injuries, may occur repeatedly in healthcare practice [3, 5]. A single seroconversion can result in chronic infection requiring lifelong antiretroviral therapy, with enduring psychosocial consequences and substantial healthcare costs [40–42]. Despite advances in experimental therapies such as gene editing and immunotherapeutic strategies, no definitive, accessible, and effective cure is currently available [40]. This burden underscores the importance of maintaining rigorous biosafety protocols and ensuring access to post-exposure prophylaxis, including after exposures perceived as low risk [41], supported not only by prevention principles but also by ethical considerations given the severity of the outcome [43]. In this context, documented seroconversions highlight the critical role of immediate PEP initiation in mitigating transmission risk [35, 37, 44].

We explored whether national HIV prevalence influenced observed seroconversion rates. Restricting analyses to studies conducted in low-prevalence settings (<1%) yielded pooled estimates similar to the main analysis. When restricting to low-prevalence studies that reported at least one seroconversion, pooled estimates increased, reflecting the contribution of observed events in higher-risk scenarios.

Overall, these findings suggest that background HIV prevalence mainly affects the probability of encountering an infected source, whereas the risk of seroconversion following exposure depends more directly on exposure severity, source-related factors, and post-exposure management. Comparisons with historical estimates such as ~ 3 per 1000 should be interpreted cautiously, as these estimates often refer to specific exposure contexts (e.g., percutaneous injuries involving confirmed HIV-positive sources) and earlier eras of prevention and surveillance [7].

Furthermore, when expressed per 1000 exposure incidents, some pooled estimates, particularly those for mucocutaneous exposures and for individuals with PEP indication who did not use PEP, fall within the same order of magnitude as historically reported benchmarks. This reinforces that occupational HIV seroconversion is rare but not negligible, especially in contexts of repeated exposure. In high-prevalence settings, no seroconversions were observed in the included studies; however, this should be interpreted cautiously given the limited number of studies and potential underreporting or follow-up constraints. Overall, these subgroup analyses indicate that background prevalence is more likely to shape exposure opportunities than to directly determine post-exposure seroconversion risk.

Most studies included in this review were conducted in high- and upper-middle-income countries, particularly in Western Europe, North America, and parts of Asia and Latin America, while Sub-Saharan Africa, despite bearing the highest global HIV burden, was underrepresented [1]. This imbalance likely reflects disparities in research funding, surveillance systems, and access to publication channels across regions [45]. Notably, all studies reporting occupational HIV seroconversion were conducted in high- and upper-middle-income settings. This concentration may relate to stronger occupational health systems, more consistent access to post-exposure testing and prophylaxis, and better infrastructure for reporting and follow-up [46, 47]. Therefore, the absence of reported cases in low-resource settings should not be interpreted as lower risk, but rather as potentially resulting from limited systematic surveillance, underreporting, and restricted access to confirmatory testing [45]. These contextual disparities must be considered when interpreting pooled estimates.

In addition, several of the few studies originating from low-resource settings presented methodological fragilities, receiving low scores in our risk of bias assessment, particularly for setting description, sample size, and follow-up clarity. These limitations may indicate selection bias, underpowered estimates, and reduced external validity. This interpretation aligns with Habibi et al. [48], who highlight that infrastructure constraints, insufficient funding, and poorly adapted health policies in low-resource settings hinder the implementation of recommended practices and robust research. Similarly, Boyd et al. [49] emphasize that limitations in electronic health record systems may exacerbate data gaps by excluding vulnerable populations. Together, these findings underscore the need to strengthen health research capacity and occupational surveillance systems in under-resourced regions to produce more representative and actionable evidence.

Beyond geographic disparities, most included studies were conducted in hospital environments, with fewer studies from dental clinics, laboratories, or home care settings. Notably, all reports of occupational HIV seroconversion originated from hospital-based studies. This concentration may represent true differences in exposure risk across settings, but it may also reflect underreporting or limitations in surveillance and follow-up infrastructure in non-hospital contexts [1, 45]. Furthermore, heterogeneity in setting descriptions and exposure scenarios, combined with the scarcity of studies outside hospitals, prevented stratified meta-analyses by healthcare setting. This limitation should be considered when interpreting pooled seroconversion rates, as findings may not fully generalize to occupational contexts characterized by different clinical practices or resource constraints.

The analysis of occupational HIV seroconversion is inherently complex due to methodological variability across studies. Although evidence from large datasets suggests a low observed incidence, differences in study design and limited description of exposure scenarios may introduce bias. In addition, the inclusion of exposures without standardized serological follow-up complicates interpretation.

As noted by Pérez-Diaz et al. [50], estimating incidence from exposure incidents rather than exclusively from those with completed follow-up may reduce reliability. Studies that include all reported exposures without adherence to standardized follow-up protocols, such as those recommended by the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) [4], risk misclassifying true outcomes and make it difficult to ascertain incidence with confidence.

When compared with the only prior global systematic review on this topic, conducted by Baggaley et al. [12], our findings suggest a lower observed risk of occupational HIV seroconversion. Baggaley’s review primarily estimated theoretical transmission probabilities using modeling approaches, incorporating empirical data when available, but it did not account for follow-up procedures, PEP use, or differences across healthcare settings.

In contrast, the present review synthesizes a broader and more recent body of observational evidence documenting seroconversion outcomes following real-world occupational exposures. By stratifying analyses by exposure type, PEP adherence, and follow-up completion, and applying statistical approaches suitable for rare events, this review provides a more detailed and clinically relevant risk profile. These differences likely reflect both methodological contrasts and temporal improvements in occupational safety practices, access to PEP, infection control awareness, and surveillance systems, reinforcing the importance of regularly updating evidence syntheses to reflect current clinical realities and inform occupational health strategies.

Examining seroconversion rates for percutaneous versus mucocutaneous exposures provides additional insight into occupational risk profiles and highlights the relevance of appropriate post-exposure management. However, risk estimation is influenced by methodological limitations and heterogeneous reporting. Studies with limited sample sizes and insufficient scenario descriptions, particularly in percutaneous cases [9, 38, 51–72], may not fully represent the realities of occupational exposure [14].

Under-reporting further complicates the assessment of seroconversion risks. Facchin et al. [73] reported that 23.76% of accidents involving biological material were not officially notified by nursing professionals in a Brazilian emergency hospital; among unreported incidents, 53.47% were percutaneous and 67.33% involved blood, with non-reporting often justified by the perception of “low risk”.

In this review, meta-analyses were conducted separately for percutaneous and mucocutaneous exposures rather than in a combined model with subgroup comparisons. This decision was based on the biological and clinical differences in transmission risk across exposure types [4, 17, 18], as well as incomplete reporting, since several studies presented data exclusively for one type of exposure. A combined model would have required assumptions incompatible with the available dataset and could have introduced structural heterogeneity and undermined interpretability.

Interestingly, the present meta-analysis observed a higher pooled incidence of seroconversion following mucocutaneous exposures compared to percutaneous exposures, a finding that contrasts with most existing literature. This unexpected result is plausibly explained by the smaller sample size in the mucocutaneous group, greater imprecision, and publication bias. In particular, publication bias was rated as “very serious” for mucocutaneous exposure studies, suggesting the possibility of inflated pooled estimates due to selective publication of positive results [26, 27]. Given these limitations, the findings regarding mucocutaneous exposures should be interpreted with caution, and future research with larger samples and standardized follow-up is warranted to clarify the true magnitude of risk.

Despite these limitations, differentiating exposure types remains essential for prevention and occupational decision-making [4, 36]. Deep percutaneous injuries should be prioritized due to higher inoculum potential, while grouping mucosal and cutaneous (non-intact skin) exposures as mucocutaneous improves comparability across studies [4, 36]. Accordingly, mucosal and cutaneous exposures were combined as mucocutaneous in this review to enhance cross-study consistency [64, 74, 75]. Future research should adopt standardized definitions and follow-up protocols to produce more precise estimates, particularly in occupational contexts where under-reporting remains a persistent challenge.

The observed difference in seroconversion rates between individuals who used PEP and those who did not, despite indication, may suggest a protective effect of this intervention. However, this finding must be interpreted cautiously. The observational design of the included studies, lack of randomization, and absence of adjustment for key confounders limit causal inference. Relevant factors include exposure severity, timing of PEP initiation, adherence, and follow-up protocols. In addition, publication bias may obscure a true assessment of PEP effectiveness in preventing occupational HIV transmission [26, 27].

Despite these limitations, prior evidence indicates that zidovudine-based PEP can substantially reduce the risk of seroconversion among healthcare workers, although protection is not absolute [36]. Uptake and adherence remain suboptimal, with fewer than 60% of individuals completing the full course, potentially compromising effectiveness [39]. This issue is particularly concerning in high-exposure settings. Cleveland et al. [76] emphasized the importance of strengthening PEP protocols among dental healthcare personnel and highlighted the role of continuous education and updated recommendations to improve adherence.

The robustness of these findings was supported by sensitivity analyses restricted to studies with low or moderate risk of bias, which yielded estimates consistent with the primary analyses across most exposure categories. In contrast, the subgroup with PEP indication but no PEP use appeared more sensitive to study quality, with observed seroconversions limited to studies at high risk of bias. This pattern underscores the importance of methodological rigor when estimating seroconversion risk and supports cautious interpretation of results derived from lower-quality evidence.

Beyond pharmacological measures, structural and behavioral strategies are essential to reduce occupational risk. Prevention of occupational HIV seroconversion depends on consistent implementation of biosafety practices, particularly correct use of PPE. Nevertheless, adherence remains limited in many healthcare settings. Noncompliance has been associated with informal employment, needle recapping, medication administration, improper disposal of sharps, insufficient biosafety training, and work overload [77–79]. Even where PPE is available, gaps in risk perception, low adherence to standard precautions, and weak reporting systems reflect persistent deficiencies in institutional safety culture [79]. Strengthening surveillance and notification systems is therefore critical [80].

Alongside clinical interpretation, the methodological quality of the evidence base warrants careful consideration. Risk of bias varied across outcomes and directly influenced pooled estimates. Risk of bias is a central determinant of reliability in evidence syntheses [81, 82], particularly for rare outcomes such as occupational HIV seroconversion. Common limitations included incomplete reporting and unclear follow-up protocols, which are recognized sources of bias that can downgrade certainty even in narrative syntheses [83]. Concerns regarding small sample sizes and insufficient setting descriptions further limited external validity [84].

For percutaneous exposures, risk of bias was generally low in most domains, particularly sample frame and outcome ascertainment, although moderate concerns persisted regarding setting description and standardized measurement. This reflects ongoing challenges in observational reporting that hinder comparability across studies [82]. In contrast, studies assessing mucocutaneous exposures more frequently exhibited high risk of bias, including small samples, inconsistent exposure definitions, and publication bias, potentially inflating seroconversion estimates [83, 85].

For PEP-related outcomes, analyses restricted to individuals who adhered to PEP showed low to moderate risk of bias, although some studies lacked detailed information on initiation timing, adherence, and follow-up [36, 76]. Among individuals with PEP indication who did not adhere, similar concerns regarding exposure classification and follow-up completeness were observed. Sensitivity analyses supported this interpretation, as pooled estimates remained stable after excluding high-risk studies for most outcomes, except in the non-PEP group, where incidence declined to zero. This suggests that observed events in this subgroup were largely driven by studies with methodological limitations. Collectively, heterogeneity in study design and domain-specific bias led to downgrading of certainty in the GRADE evaluation, resulting in very low certainty for outcomes based on all reported exposures and low certainty for most stratified analyses.

Broader methodological limitations also merit attention. Lack of standardization in post-exposure protocols, including inconsistent exposure definitions, follow-up procedures, and HIV testing strategies, likely affected comparability across studies. These discrepancies influenced risk of bias assessments, particularly domains related to exposure classification, outcome ascertainment, follow-up completeness, and statistical modeling. Given that included studies span several decades, changes in clinical practices, including the introduction and evolution of PEP, follow-up duration, and advances in HIV diagnostics, likely contributed to observed heterogeneity.

An additional consideration relates to exposures without extended serological follow-up. International guidelines recommend no further monitoring when the source is confirmed HIV-negative and the exposed individual has a negative baseline test [4, 17, 18]. In such cases, discharge reflects a clinical decision rather than loss to follow-up, and individuals are classified as not having seroconverted. To address variability in follow-up and maintain transparency, we conducted two meta-analyses, one including all reported exposures and another restricted to exposures with confirmed follow-up. This strategy allowed differentiation between robust outcome ascertainment and abbreviated monitoring, without relying on source HIV status, which was inconsistently reported and unsuitable for stratified analysis.

Despite these limitations, this review has notable methodological strengths. It was prospectively registered in PROSPERO, conducted according to PRISMA and JBI guidance, and based on a comprehensive search of multiple databases and gray literature. Dual independent screening, reviewer calibration, and standardized extraction and risk of bias assessment enhanced transparency. Stratified meta-analyses, appropriate rare-event transformations, and use of GRADE adapted for prevalence and incidence outcomes further strengthened the synthesis.

Nonetheless, weaknesses of both the review process and included studies remain. Variability in design, exposure classification, follow-up duration, and reporting clarity may have affected precision of pooled estimates. Underreporting of occupational incidents and inconsistent follow-up protocols, particularly regarding PEP, limited comparability and precluded some subgroup analyses. Lack of methodological transparency in several studies further contributed to downgrading certainty. The rarity of the outcome and predominance of zero-event studies also constrained analytical scope.

From a statistical perspective, use of the Freeman–Tukey double arcsine transformation enabled variance stabilization and comparability with prior syntheses. Although interpretability challenges at extremely low event rates have been noted [23], sensitivity analyses excluding zero-event studies yielded stable estimates, supporting robustness of the findings. Due to the analysis of a rare event, arcsine transformation was used. We recognize that standard funnel plots are of restricted interpretability when applied to arcsine- or Freeman–Tukey–transformed proportions, specifically for rare events. These transformations stabilize variances and lead to a nonlinear relationship between precision and effect size. Herein, the funnel plots provided are for exploratory purposes only. Future research may benefit from one-step approaches such as generalized linear mixed or Bayesian hierarchical models.

Overall, while limitations persist, this review provides an updated and methodologically rigorous synthesis of occupational HIV seroconversion risk and supports efforts to strengthen surveillance systems and protect healthcare workers.

Conclusion

Occupational HIV seroconversion among HCWs is rare overall, but may remain clinically relevant over a working lifetime due to repeated occupational exposures. Adherence to PEP is associated with reduced transmission risk, reinforcing the importance of prompt reporting, timely follow-up, and prevention strategies.
